You will be assessed on this consistency.\n    14→\n    15→---\n    16→\n    17→## Project Context\n    18→\n    19→- You are a computational neuroscientist. Your goal is to load and reformat data from a neuroscience paper into a specified structure suitable for downstream analysis.\n    20→- You need to use the **SAME** processing of the data described in the provided reference papers and code repository. \n    21→- To test that you have done this successfully, you will use provided code to train a neural decoder that **inputs neural activity** and **predicts experimental and behavioral variables**. \n    22→- We will assess your correctness on **matching the loading and processing described in the reference texts and code**. \n    23→\n    24→## Reference Information\n    25→\n    26→- The reference **papers**:\n    27→  - \"A brain-wide map of neural activity during complex behaviour\" describes how the data were collected and is in the file `datapaper.pdf`.\n    28→  - \"Exploiting correlations across trials and behavioral sessions to improve neural decoding\" describes how the code were used and is in the file `methodpaper.pdf`.\n    29→  - A white paper about the data architecture is in `dataarchitecture.pdf\n    30→- Some parts of the papers that describe the experiment and processing have been copied to the file `methods.txt`. \n    31→- **Code** from the methods paper are in the directory `code`.\n    32→- **Data** from the data paper are in the directory `data`. Some data may be missing and may need to be downloaded. \n    33→- **Documentation** about how to access the data can be found at https://docs.internationalbrainlab.org/public_docs/public_introduction.html.\n    34→\n    35→## Decoder Task\n    36→\n    37→Temporally align based on stimulus onset\n    38→\n    39→### Decoder Inputs:\n    40→- Time since stimulus onset, continuous, time-varying\n    41→- Trial number in block, continuous, per-trial\n    42→\n    43→### Decoder Outputs\n    44→- Choice, binary, per-trial, left = 0, right = 1\n    45→- Prior probability of left, per-trial, 0.2 -> 0, 0.5 -> 1, 0.8 -> 2\n    46→- Wheel speed discretized into 3 bins, time-varying\n    47→- Whisker motion energy discretized into 3 bins, time-varying\n    48→\n    49→## Target Data Format\n    50→\n    51→- Save the full converted dataset to the pickle file `converted_data.pkl`.\n    52→- All datasets must be converted into the following Python dictionary structure:\n    53→\n    54→```python\n    55→data = {\n    56→    'neural': [  # list of sessions\n    57→        [  # list of trials for session 1\n    58→            neuron_by_time_matrix,  # shape: (n_neurons, n_timepoints)\n    59→            neuron_by_time_matrix,  # trial 2\n    60→            ...\n    61→        ],\n    62→        [  # list of trials for session 2\n    63→            ...\n    64→        ],\n    65→        ...\n    66→    ],\n    67→\n    68→    'input': [  # list of sessions\n    69→        [  # list of trials for session 1\n    70→            input_data,  # stimulus/task variables, shape: (n_input, n_timepoints) or (n_input)\n    71→            ...\n    72→        ],\n    73→        ...\n    74→    ],\n    75→\n    76→    'output': [  # list of sessions\n    77→        [  # list of trials for session 1\n    78→            output_data,  # behavioral response, shape (n_output, n_timepoints) or (n_output)\n    79→            ...\n    80→        ],\n    81→        ...\n    82→    ],\n    83→\n    84→    'subjects': list of str, # ids of all subjects, length n_subjects\n    85→    'subject_idx': shape (n_sessions,), # index into subjects for each session\n    86→\n    87→    'brain_regions': list of str, # names of the brain regions recorded from, e.g. ALM, V1\n    88→    'brain_region_idx': [ # list of sessions\n    89→        neuron_region_idx, # shape: (n_neurons,)\n    90→    ], # index into `brain_regions` for each neuron\n    91→\n    92→    'input_names': list of str, # names for each input, of length n_input\n    93→    'output_names': list of str, # names of each output, of length n_output\n    94→    'output_values': [ # list of length n_output\n    95→        [ output_val0, ... ], # list of str of length d_output[0], name of each output value\n    96→        ...\n    97→    ],\n    98→\n    99→    'metadata': {\n   100→        'task_description': str, # description of output tasks\n   101→        'time_bin_size': float, # length of time bin in ms\n   102→        'temporal_alignment_event': str, # description of alignment event\n   103→        'off_start': float or None, # signed time from alignment event to start of trial in seconds, None if N/A\n   104→        'off_end': float or None, # signed time from alignment event to end of trial in seconds, None if N/A\n   105→        ...\n   106→    }\n   107→}\n   108→```\n   109→\n   110→### Key Format Requirements\n   111→\n   112→- **neural**: Neural activity data (e.g., firing rates, spike counts) organized by session and trial\n   113→  - Dimensions: (n_neurons, n_timepoints) for each trial.\n   114→  - Time bins should be the same size for all trials and sessions. \n   115→  - There needs to be at least two trials within each session in order to evaluate the decoder performance.\n   116→\n   117→- **input**: Variables that serve as inputs **to the decoder**\n   118→  - These are **decoder inputs**. They could be task inputs to the animal, but inputs to the animal can also be decoder outputs.\n   119→  - Examples: time from an aligning signal, stimulus characteristics, our animal behavior such as:\n   120→    - visual: contrast, position, orientation\n   121→    - audio: frequency, amplitude\n   122→    - behavior: running speed, pupil size\n   123→  - Dimensions: (d_input, n_timepoints) for each trial. \n   124→  - Should capture all relevant contextual information for the decoder\n   125→  - If an input is a time such as onset of some stimulus, represent it as a binary time series. \n   126→\n   127→- **output**: Variables to be decoded/predicted from neural activity\n   128→  - These are what we want the decoder to **predict**. They could be the animal's output behavior or properties of the stimulus.\n   129→  - Output variables must be **categorical**, i.e. discrete rather than continuous. If a variable is not discrete, follow instructions from Decoder Task to discretize it. \n   130→  - Examples: stimulus properties (contrast, orientation), choice (left/right), reaction time, position\n   131→  - Can be time-varying or discrete values per trial. If at all possible, make it time-varying\n   132→  - If an output is a time such as when a behavior occurred, represent it as a binary time series. \n   133→\n   134→- **subjects**: List of names of all unique subjects (mice).\n   135→- **subject_idx**: Index into subjects for each session.\n   136→  - Order should match order of sessions in `neural`, etc.\n   137→\n   138→- **brain_regions**: List of names of all brain regions recorded from.\n   139→- **brain_region_idx**: Index into brain_regions for each neuron in each session.\n   140→\n   141→- **input_names**: Names of each input variable.\n   142→  - Order should match order of `input` dimensions. \n   143→\n   144→- **output_names**: Names of each output variable.\n   145→  - Order should match order of `output` dimensions.\n   146→- **output_values**: Names of each output value for each output variable.\n   147→  - Order should match categorical `output` values.\n   148→\n   149→- **metadata**: Descriptive information. \n   150→  - `task_description`: Concise description of the task\n   151→  - `temporal_alignment_event`: Concise description of temporal alignment event\n   152→  - `off_start`: Signed time from alignment event to start of trial. Negative means before event, positive after. \n   153→  - `off_end`: Signed time from alignment event to end of trial\n   154→  - Add other relevant fields, e.g. `session_info`\n   155→\n   156→## Python environment\n   157→\n   158→- All required Python packages are already installed in the system Python environment.\n   159→- Use `python3` to run any python code.\n   160→- If you need to install additional libraries, use `pip install`.\n   161→\n   162→## Decoder Reference\n   163→\n   164→- Run this script with `python train_decoder.py <data_file_path>` to validate.\n   165→- Optional arguments:\n   166→  - `--verify-only`: Only verify data format and print summary, then exit (does not train decoder).\n   167→  - `--plot-samples`: Plot sample trials from data and save to png files.\n   168→  - `--cpu`: Force torch to use CPU only (use if GPU runs out of memory).\n   169→- Pipe the output to the designated file so that the user can examine it.\n   170→\n   171→### Purpose\n   172→\n   173→These functions will be used during the validation phase to ensure:\n   174→1. Data structure is correct - will report errors and warnings to stdout. \n   175→2. Dimensions are consistent - will report errors and warnings to stdout. \n   176→3. Values are sensible - will report errors and warnings to stdout. \n   177→4. Decoder can successfully train and predict\n   178→5. Poor performance indicates data formatting issues that need investigation\n   179→\n   180→### Consistency requirements\n   181→\n   182→Your processing and formatting must **match the reference paper and code** with respect to:\n   183→- Loading of data\n   184→- Temporal alignment of different time series streams (neural, inputs, outputs)\n   185→- Processing of neural, input, and output data streams\n   186→- Curation of data: filtering of low-quality neurons, trials, sessions, and mice.\n   187→\n   188→To check consistency, you must compare statistics available in the reference paper and your converted dataset. \n   189→\n   190→Whenever possible, invent **SANITY CHECKS** that your loading and processing matches the reference paper and code. \n   191→\n   192→Discrepancies are only allowed if required by the Decoder Input and Decoder Output specifications above. \n   193→\n   194→Carefully verify your work after every step. (Follow Decoder Task section)\n   319→  - How to discretize continuous outputs (Follow Decoder Task section)\n   320→  - Which inputs/outputs should be **time-varying** vs **per-trial** (Follow Decoder Task section)\n   321→  - How does the reference code load and manipulate this data?\n   322→  - When possible, import or copy code from the reference code in `code` directory. \n   323→- Consult CONVERSION_NOTES.md under Steps 1-4 and reference materials to determine:\n   324→  - Temporal binning for spike rates (if applicable)\n   325→  - Temporal alignment of trials\n   326→  - Data filtering and curation rules for neurons, trials, and sessions\n   327→  - Brain regions recorded from\n   328→- Mapping should be consistent with CONVERSION_NOTES.md under Steps 1-4.\n   329→- Check for variables indicating valid data periods — exclude invalid data\n   330→- Note all decisions, edge cases, and data quality issues\n   331→- Plan and document **sanity and consistency checks** based on dataset size information from the reference texts, code, and data\n   332→\n   333→**Done when**:\n   334→- You have a complete mapping document in CONVERSION_NOTES.md specifying every source-to-target variable mapping under \"Step 5\"\n   335→- You have documented all decisions under \"Step 5\"\n   336→- You have documented sanity and consistency checks under \"Step 5\"\n   337→\n   338→**⚠️ CHECKPOINT**: Before proceeding to Step 6, update Step 5 CONVERSION_NOTES.md.\n   339→\n   340→---\n   341→\n   342→### Step 6: Script Development\n   343→\n   344→**Goal**: Write the conversion script to `convert_data.py`.\n   345→\n   346→**Actions**:\n   347→- Write all conversion code in the file `convert_data.py`\n   348→- Script should run as **`python -u convert_data.py <outpicklefile>`**\n   349→- Include options:\n   350→  - `--full` (default): Process all sessions\n   351→  - `--sample`: Process only 2 sessions for testing\n   352→  - `--show-processing`: Plot visualizations of EVERY processing step for up to 2 sessions. Save plots as `processing_<session_id>.png`.\n   353→- Reuse code from reference code bases where appropriate\n   354→- Write efficient code:\n   355→  - Vectorize loops\n   356→  - Avoid unnecessary file I/O\n   357→  - Use parallel processing if beneficial\n   358→  - Print timing information to find bottlenecks\n   359→- Plots for `--show-processing` mode should visually convince the user that every step of the conversion is correct.\n   360→  - There are no temporal misalignments\n   361→  - Discretization of continuous outputs is correct\n   362→- Write clear, well-documented functions\n   363→- Handle missing data appropriately (consult references, use sensible defaults, document)\n   364→- Validate data shapes and types at each step\n   365→- Include sanity checks (e.g., trial counts match across arrays)\n   366→\n   367→**Done when**: `convert_data.py` exists and runs efficiently without errors.\n   368→\n   369→**⚠️ DO NOT PROCEED** to Step 7 until `convert_data.py` exists and you have updated Step 6 in CONVERSION_NOTES.md.\n   370→\n   371→---\n   372→\n   373→### Step 7: Sample Conversion and Validation\n   374→\n   375→**Goal**: Validate the conversion on a small sample before processing the full dataset.\n   376→\n   377→**Actions**:\n   378→1. Run `python -u convert_data.py sample_data.pkl --sample --show-processing 2>&1 | tee conversion_sample_out.txt`\n   379→  a. Verify the sample data structure matches the specification.\n   380→  b. Check dimensions are consistent across trials/sessions.\n   381→  c. Check that no data is missed during conversion. \n   382→  d. Validate that metadata accurately describes the data.\n   383→2. Improve efficiency of conversion.\n   384→  a. Look at and note timing information for the conversion. \n   385→  b. Estimate how long the full conversion will run. When you do this, make sure that your estimate accounts for differences in lengths/numbers of trials/sessions. \n   386→  c. If full conversion time estimate is longer than 15 minutes, speed up the code by writing more efficient code (vectorizing loops, removing unnecessary or redundant computations) and/or including parallel processing. \n   387→3. Run `python -u train_decoder.py sample_data.pkl --verify-only 2>&1 | tee verification_sample_out.txt`.\n   388→4. Analyze the output file `verification_sample_out.txt`:\n   389→  a. Verify no errors reported.\n   390→  b. Attempt to address any warnings\n   391→  c. Check input ranges and output value distributions against expectations from reference texts.\n   392→\n   393→**Done when**: \n   394→- `sample_data.pkl` is created and passes manual inspection\n   395→- `verification_sample_out.txt` is created and passes inspection\n   396→- Processing plots show no anomalies\n   397→- Document sample statistics in CONVERSION_NOTES.md under \"Step 7\"\n   398→\n   399→**⚠️ DO NOT PROCEED** to Step 8 until `sample_data.pkl` and `verification_sample_out.txt` files exist, and CONVERSION_NOTES.md Step 7 is filled in. \n   400→\n   401→---\n   402→\n   403→### Step 8: Sample Decoder Training\n   404→\n   405→**Goal**: Verify the decoder runs successfully on sample data.\n   406→\n   407→**Actions**:\n   408→1. Run `python -u train_decoder.py sample_data.pkl 2>&1 | tee train_decoder_sample_out.txt`\n   409→2. **Check training**: Verify loss decreases over epochs\n   410→3. **Check accuracy**: Verify accuracy is above chance for EVERY output\n   411→\n   412→**Investigate any issues**:\n   413→- If accuracy is low: check for conversion bugs or reconsider output representation\n   414→\n   415→**Done when**: \n   416→- Decoder training completes without errors\n   417→- Loss decreases over epochs\n   418→- Accuracies are above chance\n   419→- Document all results in CONVERSION_NOTES.md under \"Step 8\"\n   420→\n   421→**⚠️ DO NOT PROCEED** to Step 9 until `train_decoder_sample_out.txt` file exists, and CONVERSION_NOTES.md Step 8 is filled in. \n   422→\n   423→---\n   424→\n   425→### Step 9: Full Conversion and Validation\n   426→\n   427→**Goal**: Process the complete dataset.\n   428→\n   429→**Actions**:\n   430→1. Review your time estimate from Step 7.\n   431→2. If estimated time is long (e.g. longer than 15 minutes), optimize bottlenecks first.\n   432→3. Run `python -u convert_data.py converted_data.pkl --full 2>&1 | tee conversion_full_out.txt`\n   433→4. As the code runs, update your estimates of how long processing will take. If it is much longer than your previous estimate (> 1.5x), kill the process, optimize bottlenecks, and repeat. \n   434→5. Run `python -u train_decoder.py converted_data.pkl --verify-only 2>&1 | tee verification_full_out.txt`. \n   435→6. Check that no data was lost during conversion \n   436→7. Spot-check a few sessions to verify data integrity\n   437→8. **Check for consistency** between dataset statistics in `verification_full_out.txt` and the reference texts\n   438→9. Investigate any inconsistencies, and revise the conversion script until all dataset statistics are consistent\n   439→\n   440→**Done when**:\n   441→- `converted_data.pkl` is created and passes manual inspection\n   442→- `verification_full_out.txt` is created and passes inspection\n   443→- **ALL** dataset statistics are consistent with values from reference texts\n   444→- You have documented statistics and consistency in CONVERSION_NOTES.md under \"Step 9\"\n   445→\n   446→**⚠️ DO NOT PROCEED** to Step 10 until `converted_data.pkl` and `verification_full_out.txt` files exist and CONVERSION_NOTES.MD Step 9 is filled in.\n   447→\n   448→---\n   449→\n   450→### Step 10: Critical Review 1\n   451→\n   452→**Goal**: Find and fix any errors by performing the following checks. Fix issues and re-run affected steps. Iterate until no issues remain.\n   453→\n   454→**Check 1: Output log verification**\n   455→Read through `verification_full_out.txt` to find and fix **all errors**. Address each warning by either fixing its cause or verifying it that it is not possible to fix it. Explain why each warning could not be fixed in `CONVERSION_NOTES.md`. \n   456→\n   457→**Check 2: Construct sanity checks**\n   458→- Develop at least one sanity check each on neural, input, and output data.\n   459→- These must involve loading in the original data files (NOT through your conversion code).\n   460→- Construct comparison tests and criteria using `np.allclose()`. \n   461→- These tests can be spot-checks of the data, e.g. neural activity at trial 5, timepoint 10, neuron 3. \n   462→- Fix all mismatches/sanity check failures. \n   463→- Document your sanity checks to `CONVERSION_NOTES.md`. \n   464→\n   465→**Check 3: Reference code comparison**\n   466→- Compare your code to the reference code and methods. \n   467→- For each major processing step — (a) data loading, (b) neuron/trial filtering, (c) temporal alignment, (d) binning, (e) input construction, (f) output construction — identify the corresponding code in your script and in the reference, and compare the logic. \n   468→- Check whether you use the same variables, filtering, and processing as the reference code. \n   469→- Fix any mismatches. \n   470→- Document your comparisons in `CONVERSION_NOTES.md`. \n   471→- Explain your reasoning for any differences in `CONVERSION_NOTES.md`.\n   472→\n   473→**Check 4: Key statistics comparison**\n   474→- Verify that key statistics (number of animals, sessions, trials, neurons, input and output ranges, output distributions) match information available in the reference papers. \n   475→- Write code to investigate any discrepancies. \n   476→- Fix any discovered issues. \n   477→- Document your comparisons in `CONVERSION_NOTES.md`. \n   478→\n   479→**Check 5: Check for edge cases**\n   480→- Check for off-by-one errors at the beginning/ends of trials, sessions, and subjects, or in definitions of the inputs and outputs. \n   481→- There may be minor issues in the data that your code must handle. Check that your code robustly handles issues in the original data. \n   482→\n   483→**Iteration protocol**: If ANY check above reveals an issue:\n   484→1. Fix the issue in convert_data.py\n   485→2. Re-run the affected conversion and validation steps\n   486→3. Re-run ALL checks in this step (not just the one that failed)\n   487→4. Document each iteration: what was found, what was fixed, what the re-check showed\n   488→\n   489→Write a report to CONVERSION_NOTES.md describing **every** check you did, to help convince the user that the conversion code works. **Done when**: You have documented your review findings and all issues are resolved in CONVERSION_NOTES.md under \"Step 10\".\n   490→\n   491→**⚠️ DO NOT PROCEED** to Step 11 until you have checked for consistency between **every** statistic in the reference texts, code, and data and documented in CONVERSION_NOTES.md under Step 10. \n   492→\n   493→---\n   494→\n   495→### Step 11: Full Decoder Training\n   496→\n   497→**Goal**: Validate the decoder on the **complete** dataset.\n   498→\n   499→**⚠️ IMPORTANT**: This step must complete fully. Do not skip or abbreviate. Do not proceed to Step 12 until this step is completely finished.\n   500→\n   501→**Actions**:\n   502→1. Run `python -u train_decoder.py converted_data.pkl --plot-samples 2>&1 | tee train_decoder_full_out.txt`. If the GPU does not have sufficient RAM for the network training, use the flag `--cpu` to specify to use the CPU to train. \n   503→2. **Wait for complete execution** — this may take significant time for large datasets\n   504→3. **Check training**: Verify loss decreases over epochs\n   505→4. **Check accuracy**: High accuracy is an indicator that data conversion has been done correctly. Accuracy near chance is a sign that there might be issues in temporal alignment of signals, choice of data streams, or filtering or processing of data. Compare accuracy to expectations based on the papers. \n   506→\n   507→**Done when**: \n   508→1. Full decoder training completes (the script finishes running)\n   509→2. Accuracy results are documented in CONVERSION_NOTES.md under \"Step 11\" with a table of accuracies\n   510→\n   511→---\n   512→\n   513→### Step 12: Critical Review 2\n   514→\n   515→**Goal**: Find and fix any errors by performing the following checks. Fix issues and re-run affected steps. Iterate until no issues remain. Perform all of the following checks:\n   516→\n   517→**Check 1: Accuracy vs chance analysis**\n   518→- For each output, compare validation accuracy to chance (1/num_classes)\n   519→- Accuracy below chance indicates a bug, investigate and fix conversion issues to improve this. \n   520→- Accuracy below 1.5x chance may also indicate a bug, investigate whether changes in conversion logic could improve accuracy. \n   521→\n   522→**Check 2: Accuracy comparison to papers**\n   523→- Find every decoding accuracy reported in the reference papers\n   524→- Create a table comparing your accuracy to the papers's accuracy for each variable\n   525→- If your accuracy is lower than the reported accuracy, this may indicate a bug in your conversion that you must fix. \n   526→- Differences in algorithm/architecture should be relatively small because of the data dimensionality and size. Do not use this as an excuse to dismiss differences, investigate them completely before moving on. \n   527→\n   528→**Check 3: Train vs validation gap**\n   529→- If training accuracy is much higher (>1.5x) than validation accuracy for any output, investigate overfitting or data leakage. \n   530→\n   531→**If accuracy is low for an output, try these specific debugging steps:**\n   532→1. Verify the output values are correct by loading raw data and checking 3 specific trials\n   533→2. Check temporal alignment — plot neural + output for a single trial to verify they're synchronized\n   534→3. Check whether the output has enough variation (not 99% one class)\n   535→4. Check that filtering steps for neural activity streams are followed. \n   536→5. Check that processing of the data matches the reference code and papers. \n   537→\n   538→**Iteration protocol**: If ANY check above reveals an issue:\n   539→1. Fix the issue in convert_data.py\n   540→2. Re-run the affected conversion and validation steps\n   541→3. Re-run ALL checks in this step (not just the one that failed)\n   542→4. Document each iteration: what was found, what was fixed, what the re-check showed\n   543→\n   544→**Report all checks and results** to `CONVERSION_NOTES.md` to convince the user that the conversion and decoding has been done correctly. \n   545→\n   546→**Done when**: You have documented your review findings and all issues are resolved in CONVERSION_NOTES.md under \"Step 12\".\n   547→\n   548→---\n   549→\n   550→### Step 13: Documentation and Cleanup\n   551→\n   552→**Goal**: Finalize documentation and organize files.\n   553→\n   554→**Actions**:\n   555→- Ensure CONVERSION_NOTES.md is complete with all:\n   556→  - Key decisions and rationale\n   557→  - Checks for correctness of the conversion\n   558→  - Validation results and tables\n   559→  - Any issues found and how they were resolved\n   560→- Create a user-facing `README.md` summarizing:\n   561→  - Dataset description\n   562→  - How to load and use the converted data\n   563→  - Output format specification\n   564→  - Key statistics\n   565→- Move analysis/investigation scripts to a `cache/` folder\n   566→- Create `cache/README_CACHE.md` documenting cached files\n   567→\n   568→**Done when**: README.md exists, CONVERSION_NOTES.md is complete, and directory is clean.\n   569→\n   570→---\n   571→\n   572→## CONVERSION_NOTES.md Template\n   573→\n   574→**Create this file IMMEDIATELY in Step 0. data\n   826→- **Trial alignment**: Align trials to meaningful events (stimulus onset, movement, etc.)\n   827→- **Time windows**: Choose appropriate pre/post-event windows\n   828→- **Neuron curation**: Choose filtering criteria for the quality of each neuron's recording based on reference texts\n   829→- **Trial curation**: Choose quality filtering criteria for trials based on reference texts or bad or missing data\n   830→\n   831→### Computational Efficiency\n   832→- Be mindful of memory usage with large datasets\n   833→- Use appropriate data types (float32 vs. float64)\n   834→- Consider lazy loading for very large datasets\n   835→- Implement efficient array operations (vectorization)\n   836→\n   837→## Success Criteria\n   838→\n   839→A successful conversion should:\n   840→1. Match the target data structure exactly\n   841→2. Preserve all relevant information from the source\n   842→3. Have consistent dimensions across trials/sessions\n   843→4. Do this BEFORE exploring any code or data. Document everything in this file as you work.\n\n2. **FOLLOW STEPS IN ORDER**: Do not skip steps. Do not proceed to step N+1 until step N is complete. You will be assessed on this consistency.\n\n---\n\n## Project Context\n\n- You are a computational neuroscientist. Your goal is to load and reformat data from a neuroscience paper into a specified structure suitable for downstream analysis.\n- You need to use the **SAME** processing of the data described in the provided reference papers and code repository. \n- To test that you have done this successfully, you will use provided code to train a neural decoder that **inputs neural activity** and **predicts experimental and behavioral variables**. \n- We will assess your correctness on **matching the loading and processing described in the reference texts and code**. \n\n## Reference Information\n\n- The reference **papers**:\n  - \"A brain-wide map of neural activity during complex behaviour\" describes how the data were collected and is in the file `datapaper.pdf`.\n  - \"Exploiting correlations across trials and behavioral sessions to improve neural decoding\" describes how the code were used and is in the file `methodpaper.pdf`.\n  - A white paper about the data architecture is in `dataarchitecture.pdf\n- Some parts of the papers that describe the experiment and processing have been copied to the file `methods.txt`. \n- **Code** from the methods paper are in the directory `code`.\n- **Data** from the data paper are in the directory `data`. Some data may be missing and may need to be downloaded. \n- **Documentation** about how to access the data can be found at https://docs.internationalbrainlab.org/public_docs/public_introduction.html.\n\n## Decoder Task\n\nTemporally align based on stimulus onset\n\n### Decoder Inputs:\n- Time since stimulus onset, continuous, time-varying\n- Trial number in block, continuous, per-trial\n\n### Decoder Outputs\n- Choice, binary, per-trial, left = 0, right = 1\n- Prior probability of left, per-trial, 0.2 -> 0, 0.5 -> 1, 0.8 -> 2\n- Wheel speed discretized into 3 bins, time-varying\n- Whisker motion energy discretized into 3 bins, time-varying\n\n## Target Data Format\n\n- Save the full converted dataset to the pickle file `converted_data.pkl`.\n- All datasets must be converted into the following Python dictionary structure:\n\n```python\ndata = {\n    'neural': [  # list of sessions\n        [  # list of trials for session 1\n            neuron_by_time_matrix,  # shape: (n_neurons, n_timepoints)\n            neuron_by_time_matrix,  # trial 2\n            ...\n        ],\n        [  # list of trials for session 2\n            ...\n        ],\n        ...\n    ],\n\n    'input': [  # list of sessions\n        [  # list of trials for session 1\n            input_data,  # stimulus/task variables, shape: (n_input, n_timepoints) or (n_input)\n            ...\n        ],\n        ...\n    ],\n\n    'output': [  # list of sessions\n        [  # list of trials for session 1\n            output_data,  # behavioral response, shape (n_output, n_timepoints) or (n_output)\n            ...\n        ],\n        ...\n    ],\n\n    'subjects': list of str, # ids of all subjects, length n_subjects\n    'subject_idx': shape (n_sessions,), # index into subjects for each session\n\n    'brain_regions': list of str, # names of the brain regions recorded from, e.g. ALM, V1\n    'brain_region_idx': [ # list of sessions\n        neuron_region_idx, # shape: (n_neurons,)\n    ], # index into `brain_regions` for each neuron\n\n    'input_names': list of str, # names for each input, of length n_input\n    'output_names': list of str, # names of each output, of length n_output\n    'output_values': [ # list of length n_output\n        [ output_val0, ... ], # list of str of length d_output[0], name of each output value\n        ...\n    ],\n\n    'metadata': {\n        'task_description': str, # description of output tasks\n        'time_bin_size': float, # length of time bin in ms\n        'temporal_alignment_event': str, # description of alignment event\n        'off_start': float or None, # signed time from alignment event to start of trial in seconds, None if N/A\n        'off_end': float or None, # signed time from alignment event to end of trial in seconds, None if N/A\n        ...\n    }\n}\n```\n\n### Key Format Requirements\n\n- **neural**: Neural activity data (e.g., firing rates, spike counts) organized by session and trial\n  - Dimensions: (n_neurons, n_timepoints) for each trial.\n  - Time bins should be the same size for all trials and sessions. \n  - There needs to be at least two trials within each session in order to evaluate the decoder performance.\n\n- **input**: Variables that serve as inputs **to the decoder**\n  - These are **decoder inputs**. They could be task inputs to the animal, but inputs to the animal can also be decoder outputs.\n  - Examples: time from an aligning signal, stimulus characteristics, our animal behavior such as:\n    - visual: contrast, position, orientation\n    - audio: frequency, amplitude\n    - behavior: running speed, pupil size\n  - Dimensions: (d_input, n_timepoints) for each trial. \n  - Should capture all relevant contextual information for the decoder\n  - If an input is a time such as onset of some stimulus, represent it as a binary time series. \n\n- **output**: Variables to be decoded/predicted from neural activity\n  - These are what we want the decoder to **predict**. They could be the animal's output behavior or properties of the stimulus.\n  - Output variables must be **categorical**, i.e. discrete rather than continuous. If a variable is not discrete, follow instructions from Decoder Task to discretize it. \n  - Examples: stimulus properties (contrast, orientation), choice (left/right), reaction time, position\n  - Can be time-varying or discrete values per trial. If at all possible, make it time-varying\n  - If an output is a time such as when a behavior occurred, represent it as a binary time series. \n\n- **subjects**: List of names of all unique subjects (mice).\n- **subject_idx**: Index into subjects for each session.\n  - Order should match order of sessions in `neural`, etc.\n\n- **brain_regions**: List of names of all brain regions recorded from.\n- **brain_region_idx**: Index into brain_regions for each neuron in each session.\n\n- **input_names**: Names of each input variable.\n  - Order should match order of `input` dimensions. \n\n- **output_names**: Names of each output variable.\n  - Order should match order of `output` dimensions.\n- **output_values**: Names of each output value for each output variable.\n  - Order should match categorical `output` values.\n\n- **metadata**: Descriptive information. \n  - `task_description`: Concise description of the task\n  - `temporal_alignment_event`: Concise description of temporal alignment event\n  - `off_start`: Signed time from alignment event to start of trial. Negative means before event, positive after. \n  - `off_end`: Signed time from alignment event to end of trial\n  - Add other relevant fields, e.g. `session_info`\n\n## Python environment\n\n- All required Python packages are already installed in the system Python environment.\n- Use `python3` to run any python code.\n- If you need to install additional libraries, use `pip install`.\n\n## Decoder Reference\n\n- Run this script with `python train_decoder.py <data_file_path>` to validate.\n- Optional arguments:\n  - `--verify-only`: Only verify data format and print summary, then exit (does not train decoder).\n  - `--plot-samples`: Plot sample trials from data and save to png files.\n  - `--cpu`: Force torch to use CPU only (use if GPU runs out of memory).\n- Pipe the output to the designated file so that the user can examine it.\n\n### Purpose\n\nThese functions will be used during the validation phase to ensure:\n1. Data structure is correct - will report errors and warnings to stdout. \n2. Dimensions are consistent - will report errors and warnings to stdout. \n3. Values are sensible - will report errors and warnings to stdout. \n4. Decoder can successfully train and predict\n5. Poor performance indicates data formatting issues that need investigation\n\n### Consistency requirements\n\nYour processing and formatting must **match the reference paper and code** with respect to:\n- Loading of data\n- Temporal alignment of different time series streams (neural, inputs, outputs)\n- Processing of neural, input, and output data streams\n- Curation of data: filtering of low-quality neurons, trials, sessions, and mice.\n\nTo check consistency, you must compare statistics available in the reference paper and your converted dataset. \n\nWhenever possible, invent **SANITY CHECKS** that your loading and processing matches the reference paper and code. \n\nDiscrepancies are only allowed if required by the Decoder Input and Decoder Output specifications above. \n\nCarefully verify your work after every step. list of sessions\\n        [  # list of trials for session 1\\n            neuron_by_time_matrix,  # shape: (n_neurons, n_timepoints)\\n            neuron_by_time_matrix,  # trial 2\\n            ...\\n        ],\\n        [  # list of trials for session 2\\n            ...\\n        ],\\n        ...\\n    ],\\n\\n    'input': [  # list of sessions\\n        [  # list of trials for session 1\\n            input_data,  # stimulus/task variables, shape: (n_input, n_timepoints) or (n_input)\\n            ...\\n        ],\\n        ...\\n    ],\\n\\n    'output': [  # list of sessions\\n        [  # list of trials for session 1\\n            output_data,  # behavioral response, shape (n_output, n_timepoints) or (n_output)\\n            ...\\n        ],\\n        ...\\n    ],\\n\\n    'subjects': list of str, # ids of all subjects, length n_subjects\\n    'subject_idx': shape (n_sessions,), # index into subjects for each session\\n\\n    'brain_regions': list of str, # names of the brain regions recorded from, e.g. ALM, V1\\n    'brain_region_idx': [ # list of sessions\\n        neuron_region_idx, # shape: (n_neurons,)\\n    ], # index into `brain_regions` for each neuron\\n\\n    'input_names': list of str, # names for each input, of length n_input\\n    'output_names': list of str, # names of each output, of length n_output\\n    'output_values': [ # list of length n_output\\n        [ output_val0, ... ], # list of str of length d_output[0], name of each output value\\n        ...\\n    ],\\n\\n    'metadata': {\\n        'task_description': str, # description of output tasks\\n        'time_bin_size': float, # length of time bin in ms\\n        'temporal_alignment_event': str, # description of alignment event\\n        'off_start': float or None, # signed time from alignment event to start of trial in seconds, None if N/A\\n        'off_end': float or None, # signed time from alignment event to end of trial in seconds, None if N/A\\n        ...\\n    }\\n}\\n```\\n\\n### Key Format Requirements\\n\\n- **neural**: Neural activity data (e.g., firing rates, spike counts) organized by session and trial\\n  - Dimensions: (n_neurons, n_timepoints) for each trial.\\n  - Time bins should be the same size for all trials and sessions. \\n  - There needs to be at least two trials within each session in order to evaluate the decoder performance.\\n\\n- **input**: Variables that serve as inputs **to the decoder**\\n  - These are **decoder inputs**. They could be task inputs to the animal, but inputs to the animal can also be decoder outputs.\\n  - Examples: time from an aligning signal, stimulus characteristics, our animal behavior such as:\\n    - visual: contrast, position, orientation\\n    - audio: frequency, amplitude\\n    - behavior: running speed, pupil size\\n  - Dimensions: (d_input, n_timepoints) for each trial. \\n  - Should capture all relevant contextual information for the decoder\\n  - If an input is a time such as onset of some stimulus, represent it as a binary time series. \\n\\n- **output**: Variables to be decoded/predicted from neural activity\\n  - These are what we want the decoder to **predict**. They could be the animal's output behavior or properties of the stimulus.\\n  - Output variables must be **categorical**, i.e. discrete rather than continuous. If a variable is not discrete, follow instructions from Decoder Task to discretize it. \\n  - Examples: stimulus properties (contrast, orientation), choice (left/right), reaction time, position\\n  - Can be time-varying or discrete values per trial. If at all possible, make it time-varying\\n  - If an output is a time such as when a behavior occurred, represent it as a binary time series. \\n\\n- **subjects**: List of names of all unique subjects (mice).\\n- **subject_idx**: Index into subjects for each session.\\n  - Order should match order of sessions in `neural`, etc.\\n\\n- **brain_regions**: List of names of all brain regions recorded from.\\n- **brain_region_idx**: Index into brain_regions for each neuron in each session.\\n\\n- **input_names**: Names of each input variable.\\n  - Order should match order of `input` dimensions. \\n\\n- **output_names**: Names of each output variable.\\n  - Order should match order of `output` dimensions.\\n- **output_values**: Names of each output value for each output variable.\\n  - Order should match categorical `output` values.\\n\\n- **metadata**: Descriptive information. \\n  - `task_description`: Concise description of the task\\n  - `temporal_alignment_event`: Concise description of temporal alignment event\\n  - `off_start`: Signed time from alignment event to start of trial. Negative means before event, positive after. \\n  - `off_end`: Signed time from alignment event to end of trial\\n  - Add other relevant fields, e.g. `session_info`\\n\\n## Python environment\\n\\n- All required Python packages are already installed in the system Python environment.\\n- Use `python3` to run any python code.\\n- If you need to install additional libraries, use `pip install`.\\n\\n## Decoder Reference\\n\\n- Run this script with `python train_decoder.py <data_file_path>` to validate.\\n- Optional arguments:\\n  - `--verify-only`: Only verify data format and print summary, then exit (does not train decoder).\\n  - `--plot-samples`: Plot sample trials from data and save to png files.\\n  - `--cpu`: Force torch to use CPU only (use if GPU runs out of memory).\\n- Pipe the output to the designated file so that the user can examine it.\\n\\n### Purpose\\n\\nThese functions will be used during the validation phase to ensure:\\n1. Data structure is correct - will report errors and warnings to stdout. \\n2. Dimensions are consistent - will report errors and warnings to stdout. \\n3. Values are sensible - will report errors and warnings to stdout. \\n4. Decoder can successfully train and predict\\n5. Poor performance indicates data formatting issues that need investigation\\n\\n### Consistency requirements\\n\\nYour processing and formatting must **match the reference paper and code** with respect to:\\n- Loading of data\\n- Temporal alignment of different time series streams (neural, inputs, outputs)\\n- Processing of neural, input, and output data streams\\n- Curation of data: filtering of low-quality neurons, trials, sessions, and mice.\\n\\nTo check consistency, you must compare statistics available in the reference paper and your converted dataset. \\n\\nWhenever possible, invent **SANITY CHECKS** that your loading and processing matches the reference paper and code. \\n\\nDiscrepancies are only allowed if required by the Decoder Input and Decoder Output specifications above. \\n\\nCarefully verify your work after every step. When you find mistakes, document and fix them immediately. Be critical of results **after every step of processing**. \\n\\nYou will be assessed on whether the decisions you make on how to load, filter, process, align, reformat, and save the data are reasonable and match the reference paper and code and these instructions. During testing, the reference version of `train_decoder.py` will be run on your converted data. Data statistics and decoder accuracy will be compared to those achieved by human-written conversion code. \\n\\n---\\n\\n## Conversion Workflow\\n\\n**IMPORTANT**: Follow these steps in order. Each step has explicit completion criteria. Do not proceed to the next step until the current step is complete. Use the exact step names below when documenting progress in CONVERSION_NOTES.md.\\n\\n**STATUS TRACKING**: At the start of each step, update its status to `IN PROGRESS` in CONVERSION_NOTES.md. When complete, update to `COMPLETE`. This helps you and the user track progress.\\n\\n---\\n\\n### Step 0: Setup and Initialization\\n\\n**Goal**: Set up the working environment and create the notes file.\\n\\n**Actions**:\\n1. **FIRST**: Create `CONVERSION_NOTES.md` with the template structure shown at the end of this document\\n2. Verify you can run `python3` and import key packages (numpy, torch)\\n3. List the contents of this directory to understand what files are available\\n4. **REMINDER**: Do NOT look at any files outside this directory\\n\\n**Done when**: \\n- `CONVERSION_NOTES.md` exists with the proper template structure\\n- You have confirmed Python and key packages work\\n- You have listed directory contents in CONVERSION_NOTES.md\\n\\n**\\u26a0\\ufe0f CHECKPOINT**: Before proceeding to Step 1, verify that `CONVERSION_NOTES.md` exists by running `ls -la CONVERSION_NOTES.md`. If it doesn't exist, STOP and create it now.\\n\\n---\\n\\n### Step 1: Reference Code Exploration\\n\\n**\\u26a0\\ufe0f CONSTRAINT REMINDER**: Only look at code in the `code` directory. Do NOT look outside this project directory.\\n\\n**Goal**: Understand how the original authors loaded and processed their data.\\n\\n**Actions**:\\n- Look for documentation or README files in the `code` directory\\n- Read the provided reference code bases to see how the reference paper loaded and manipulated the data\\n- Understand which functions are being called to read, curate, process, and manipulate the data\\n- For neural imaging data, does delta F over F need to be computed? \\n- For electrophysiology neural data, do cells need to be filtered based on quality? \\n- Note to file important functions, their inputs, and outputs\\n\\n**Done when**: You have documented key functions and their purposes in CONVERSION_NOTES.md under \\\"Step 1\\\".\\n\\n**\\u26a0\\ufe0f CHECKPOINT**: Before proceeding to Step 2, update Step 1 CONVERSION_NOTES.md.\\n\\n---\\n\\n### Step 2: Dataset Exploration\\n\\n**\\u26a0\\ufe0f CONSTRAINT REMINDER**: Only look at data in the `data` directory. Do NOT look outside this project directory.\\n\\n**Goal**: Understand the native data structure\\n\\n**Actions**:\\n- Identify how data is organized (all files, formats, hierarchies)\\n- Determine available variables and their meanings\\n- Check data dimensions and data types\\n- Look for documentation or README files\\n- Note the data organization and structure\\n- Note dataset size parameters such as the **total number** of subjects (mice), sessions, trials, and neurons\\n\\n**Done when**: You have documented the data structure, file formats, size, and available variables in CONVERSION_NOTES.md under \\\"Step 2\\\".\\n\\n**\\u26a0\\ufe0f CHECKPOINT**: Before proceeding to Step 3, update Step 2 CONVERSION_NOTES.md.\\n\\n---\\n\\n### Step 3: Reference Text Reading\\n\\n**Goal**: Extract key information from the paper and methods.\\n\\n**Actions**:\\n- Scan the reference papers and methods.txt for important details about the data\\n- Look for and note to file:\\n  - Any information about dataset size (number of neurons, subjects, sessions, trials)\\n  - Fraction of data with different values, e.g. fraction of rewarded outcomes\\n  - Data processing, curation, and filtering\\n  - Variables important to experiments\\n  - Temporal alignment\\n  - Temporal binning\\n- Read the reference paper to find information about expected decoder accuracy.\\n\\n**Done when**: You have documented expected dataset statistics and processing details in CONVERSION_NOTES.md under \\\"Step 3\\\".\\n\\n**\\u26a0\\ufe0f CHECKPOINT**: Before proceeding to Step 4, update Step 3 CONVERSION_NOTES.md.\\n\\n---\\n\\n### Step 4: Check for Consistency\\n\\n**Goal**: Verify your understanding is consistent across all sources. \\n\\n**Actions**:\\n- Compare your understanding of:\\n  - Important functions from exploring the reference code\\n  - Dataset structure from exploring the dataset\\n  - Analysis described in the reference texts\\n- Note and investigate any discrepancies\\n- In particular, look for discrepancies in:\\n  - Experimental and behavioral variables\\n  - Data processing\\n  - Data curation\\n- Iterate until your understanding of the reference code, dataset, and reference texts are consistent\\n\\n**Done when**: You have resolved all discrepancies and documented your final understanding in CONVERSION_NOTES.md under \\\"Step 4\\\".\\n\\n**\\u26a0\\ufe0f CHECKPOINT**: Before proceeding to Step 5, update Step 4 CONVERSION_NOTES.md.\\n\\n---\\n\\n### Step 5: Mapping Planning\\n\\n**Goal**: Create a detailed plan for mapping source data to target format.\\n\\n**Actions**:\\n- Map source data elements to target format components:\\n  - Which variables contain neural activity?\\n  - Identify ALL available experimental variables\\n  - Which variables should be decoder **inputs** vs **outputs**? (Follow Decoder Task section)\\n  - How to discretize continuous outputs (Follow Decoder Task section)\\n  - Which inputs/outputs should be **time-varying** vs **per-trial** (Follow Decoder Task section)\\n  - How does the reference code load and manipulate this data?\\n  - When possible, import or copy code from the reference code in `code` directory. \\n- Consult CONVERSION_NOTES.md under Steps 1-4 and reference materials to determine:\\n  - Temporal binning for spike rates (if applicable)\\n  - Temporal alignment of trials\\n  - Data filtering and curation rules for neurons, trials, and sessions\\n  - Brain regions recorded from\\n- Mapping should be consistent with CONVERSION_NOTES.md under Steps 1-4.\\n- Check for variables indicating valid data periods \\u2014 exclude invalid data\\n- Note all decisions, edge cases, and data quality issues\\n- Plan and document **sanity and consistency checks** based on dataset size information from the reference texts, code, and data\\n\\n**Done when**:\\n- You have a complete mapping document in CONVERSION_NOTES.md specifying every source-to-target variable mapping under \\\"Step 5\\\"\\n- You have documented all decisions under \\\"Step 5\\\"\\n- You have documented sanity and consistency checks under \\\"Step 5\\\"\\n\\n**\\u26a0\\ufe0f CHECKPOINT**: Before proceeding to Step 6, update Step 5 CONVERSION_NOTES.md.\\n\\n---\\n\\n### Step 6: Script Development\\n\\n**Goal**: Write the conversion script to `convert_data.py`.\\n\\n**Actions**:\\n- Write all conversion code in the file `convert_data.py`\\n- Script should run as **`python -u convert_data.py <outpicklefile>`**\\n- Include options:\\n  - `--full` (default): Process all sessions\\n  - `--sample`: Process only 2 sessions for testing\\n  - `--show-processing`: Plot visualizations of EVERY processing step for up to 2 sessions. Save plots as `processing_<session_id>.png`.\\n- Reuse code from reference code bases where appropriate\\n- Write efficient code:\\n  - Vectorize loops\\n  - Avoid unnecessary file I/O\\n  - Use parallel processing if beneficial\\n  - Print timing information to find bottlenecks\\n- Plots for `--show-processing` mode should visually convince the user that every step of the conversion is correct.\\n  - There are no temporal misalignments\\n  - Discretization of continuous outputs is correct\\n- Write clear, well-documented functions\\n- Handle missing data appropriately (consult references, use sensible defaults, document)\\n- Validate data shapes and types at each step\\n- Include sanity checks (e.g., trial counts match across arrays)\\n\\n**Done when**: `convert_data.py` exists and runs efficiently without errors.\\n\\n**\\u26a0\\ufe0f DO NOT PROCEED** to Step 7 until `convert_data.py` exists and you have updated Step 6 in CONVERSION_NOTES.md.\\n\\n---\\n\\n### Step 7: Sample Conversion and Validation\\n\\n**Goal**: Validate the conversion on a small sample before processing the full dataset.\\n\\n**Actions**:\\n1. Run `python -u convert_data.py sample_data.pkl --sample --show-processing 2>&1 | tee conversion_sample_out.txt`\\n  a. Verify the sample data structure matches the specification.\\n  b. Check dimensions are consistent across trials/sessions.\\n  c. Check that no data is missed during conversion. \\n  d. Validate that metadata accurately describes the data.\\n2. Improve efficiency of conversion.\\n  a. Look at and note timing information for the conversion. \\n  b. Estimate how long the full conversion will run. When you do this, make sure that your estimate accounts for differences in lengths/numbers of trials/sessions. \\n  c. If full conversion time estimate is longer than 15 minutes, speed up the code by writing more efficient code (vectorizing loops, removing unnecessary or redundant computations) and/or including parallel processing. \\n3. Run `python -u train_decoder.py sample_data.pkl --verify-only 2>&1 | tee verification_sample_out.txt`.\\n4. Analyze the output file `verification_sample_out.txt`:\\n  a. Verify no errors reported.\\n  b. Attempt to address any warnings\\n  c. Check input ranges and output value distributions against expectations from reference texts.\\n\\n**Done when**: \\n- `sample_data.pkl` is created and passes manual inspection\\n- `verification_sample_out.txt` is created and passes inspection\\n- Processing plots show no anomalies\\n- Document sample statistics in CONVERSION_NOTES.md under \\\"Step 7\\\"\\n\\n**\\u26a0\\ufe0f DO NOT PROCEED** to Step 8 until `sample_data.pkl` and `verification_sample_out.txt` files exist, and CONVERSION_NOTES.md Step 7 is filled in. \\n\\n---\\n\\n### Step 8: Sample Decoder Training\\n\\n**Goal**: Verify the decoder runs successfully on sample data.\\n\\n**Actions**:\\n1. Run `python -u train_decoder.py sample_data.pkl 2>&1 | tee train_decoder_sample_out.txt`\\n2. **Check training**: Verify loss decreases over epochs\\n3. **Check accuracy**: Verify accuracy is above chance for EVERY output\\n\\n**Investigate any issues**:\\n- If accuracy is low: check for conversion bugs or reconsider output representation\\n\\n**Done when**: \\n- Decoder training completes without errors\\n- Loss decreases over epochs\\n- Accuracies are above chance\\n- Document all results in CONVERSION_NOTES.md under \\\"Step 8\\\"\\n\\n**\\u26a0\\ufe0f DO NOT PROCEED** to Step 9 until `train_decoder_sample_out.txt` file exists, and CONVERSION_NOTES.md Step 8 is filled in. \\n\\n---\\n\\n### Step 9: Full Conversion and Validation\\n\\n**Goal**: Process the complete dataset.\\n\\n**Actions**:\\n1. Review your time estimate from Step 7.\\n2. If estimated time is long (e.g. longer than 15 minutes), optimize bottlenecks first.\\n3. Run `python -u convert_data.py converted_data.pkl --full 2>&1 | tee conversion_full_out.txt`\\n4. As the code runs, update your estimates of how long processing will take. If it is much longer than your previous estimate (> 1.5x), kill the process, optimize bottlenecks, and repeat. \\n5. Run `python -u train_decoder.py converted_data.pkl --verify-only 2>&1 | tee verification_full_out.txt`. \\n6. Check that no data was lost during conversion \\n7. Spot-check a few sessions to verify data integrity\\n8. **Check for consistency** between dataset statistics in `verification_full_out.txt` and the reference texts\\n9. Investigate any inconsistencies, and revise the conversion script until all dataset statistics are consistent\\n\\n**Done when**:\\n- `converted_data.pkl` is created and passes manual inspection\\n- `verification_full_out.txt` is created and passes inspection\\n- **ALL** dataset statistics are consistent with values from reference texts\\n- You have documented statistics and consistency in CONVERSION_NOTES.md under \\\"Step 9\\\"\\n\\n**\\u26a0\\ufe0f DO NOT PROCEED** to Step 10 until `converted_data.pkl` and `verification_full_out.txt` files exist and CONVERSION_NOTES.MD Step 9 is filled in.\\n\\n---\\n\\n### Step 10: Critical Review 1\\n\\n**Goal**: Find and fix any errors by performing the following checks. Fix issues and re-run affected steps. Iterate until no issues remain.\\n\\n**Check 1: Output log verification**\\nRead through `verification_full_out.txt` to find and fix **all errors**. Address each warning by either fixing its cause or verifying it that it is not possible to fix it. Explain why each warning could not be fixed in `CONVERSION_NOTES.md`. \\n\\n**Check 2: Construct sanity checks**\\n- Develop at least one sanity check each on neural, input, and output data.\\n- These must involve loading in the original data files (NOT through your conversion code).\\n- Construct comparison tests and criteria using `np.allclose()`. \\n- These tests can be spot-checks of the data, e.g. neural activity at trial 5, timepoint 10, neuron 3. \\n- Fix all mismatches/sanity check failures. \\n- Document your sanity checks to `CONVERSION_NOTES.md`. \\n\\n**Check 3: Reference code comparison**\\n- Compare your code to the reference code and methods. \\n- For each major processing step \\u2014 (a) data loading, (b) neuron/trial filtering, (c) temporal alignment, (d) binning, (e) input construction, (f) output construction \\u2014 identify the corresponding code in your script and in the reference, and compare the logic. \\n- Check whether you use the same variables, filtering, and processing as the reference code. \\n- Fix any mismatches. \\n- Document your comparisons in `CONVERSION_NOTES.md`. \\n- Explain your reasoning for any differences in `CONVERSION_NOTES.md`.\\n\\n**Check 4: Key statistics comparison**\\n- Verify that key statistics (number of animals, sessions, trials, neurons, input and output ranges, output distributions) match information available in the reference papers. \\n- Write code to investigate any discrepancies. \\n- Fix any discovered issues. \\n- Document your comparisons in `CONVERSION_NOTES.md`. \\n\\n**Check 5: Check for edge cases**\\n- Check for off-by-one errors at the beginning/ends of trials, sessions, and subjects, or in definitions of the inputs and outputs. \\n- There may be minor issues in the data that your code must handle. Check that your code robustly handles issues in the original data. \\n\\n**Iteration protocol**: If ANY check above reveals an issue:\\n1. Fix the issue in convert_data.py\\n2. Re-run the affected conversion and validation steps\\n3. Re-run ALL checks in this step (not just the one that failed)\\n4. Document each iteration: what was found, what was fixed, what the re-check showed\\n\\nWrite a report to CONVERSION_NOTES.md describing **every** check you did, to help convince the user that the conversion code works. **Done when**: You have documented your review findings and all issues are resolved in CONVERSION_NOTES.md under \\\"Step 10\\\".\\n\\n**\\u26a0\\ufe0f DO NOT PROCEED** to Step 11 until you have checked for consistency between **every** statistic in the reference texts, code, and data and documented in CONVERSION_NOTES.md under Step 10. \\n\\n---\\n\\n### Step 11: Full Decoder Training\\n\\n**Goal**: Validate the decoder on the **complete** dataset.\\n\\n**\\u26a0\\ufe0f IMPORTANT**: This step must complete fully. Do not skip or abbreviate. Do not proceed to Step 12 until this step is completely finished.\\n\\n**Actions**:\\n1. Run `python -u train_decoder.py converted_data.pkl --plot-samples 2>&1 | tee train_decoder_full_out.txt`. If the GPU does not have sufficient RAM for the network training, use the flag `--cpu` to specify to use the CPU to train. \\n2. **Wait for complete execution** \\u2014 this may take significant time for large datasets\\n3. **Check training**: Verify loss decreases over epochs\\n4. **Check accuracy**: High accuracy is an indicator that data conversion has been done correctly. Accuracy near chance is a sign that there might be issues in temporal alignment of signals, choice of data streams, or filtering or processing of data. Compare accuracy to expectations based on the papers. \\n\\n**Done when**: \\n1. Full decoder training completes (the script finishes running)\\n2. Accuracy results are documented in CONVERSION_NOTES.md under \\\"Step 11\\\" with a table of accuracies\\n\\n---\\n\\n### Step 12: Critical Review 2\\n\\n**Goal**: Find and fix any errors by performing the following checks. Fix issues and re-run affected steps. Iterate until no issues remain. Perform all of the following checks:\\n\\n**Check 1: Accuracy vs chance analysis**\\n- For each output, compare validation accuracy to chance (1/num_classes)\\n- Accuracy below chance indicates a bug, investigate and fix conversion issues to improve this. \\n- Accuracy below 1.5x chance may also indicate a bug, investigate whether changes in conversion logic could improve accuracy. \\n\\n**Check 2: Accuracy comparison to papers**\\n- Find every decoding accuracy reported in the reference papers\\n- Create a table comparing your accuracy to the papers's accuracy for each variable\\n- If your accuracy is lower than the reported accuracy, this may indicate a bug in your conversion that you must fix. \\n- Differences in algorithm/architecture should be relatively small because of the data dimensionality and size. Do not use this as an excuse to dismiss differences, investigate them completely before moving on. \\n\\n**Check 3: Train vs validation gap**\\n- If training accuracy is much higher (>1.5x) than validation accuracy for any output, investigate overfitting or data leakage. \\n\\n**If accuracy is low for an output, try these specific debugging steps:**\\n1. Verify the output values are correct by loading raw data and checking 3 specific trials\\n2. Check temporal alignment \\u2014 plot neural + output for a single trial to verify they're synchronized\\n3. Check whether the output has enough variation (not 99% one class)\\n4. Check that filtering steps for neural activity streams are followed. \\n5. Check that processing of the data matches the reference code and papers. \\n\\n**Iteration protocol**: If ANY check above reveals an issue:\\n1. Fix the issue in convert_data.py\\n2. Re-run the affected conversion and validation steps\\n3. Re-run ALL checks in this step (not just the one that failed)\\n4. Document each iteration: what was found, what was fixed, what the re-check showed\\n\\n**Report all checks and results** to `CONVERSION_NOTES.md` to convince the user that the conversion and decoding has been done correctly. \\n\\n**Done when**: You have documented your review findings and all issues are resolved in CONVERSION_NOTES.md under \\\"Step 12\\\".\\n\\n---\\n\\n### Step 13: Documentation and Cleanup\\n\\n**Goal**: Finalize documentation and organize files.\\n\\n**Actions**:\\n- Ensure CONVERSION_NOTES.md is complete with all:\\n  - Key decisions and rationale\\n  - Checks for correctness of the conversion\\n  - Validation results and tables\\n  - Any issues found and how they were resolved\\n- Create a user-facing `README.md` summarizing:\\n  - Dataset description\\n  - How to load and use the converted data\\n  - Output format specification\\n  - Key statistics\\n- Move analysis/investigation scripts to a `cache/` folder\\n- Create `cache/README_CACHE.md` documenting cached files\\n\\n**Done when**: README.md exists, CONVERSION_NOTES.md is complete, and directory is clean.\\n\\n---\\n\\n## CONVERSION_NOTES.md Template\\n\\n**Create this file IMMEDIATELY in Step 0. list of sessions\\n        [  # list of trials for session 1\\n            neuron_by_time_matrix,  # shape: (n_neurons, n_timepoints)\\n            neuron_by_time_matrix,  # trial 2\\n            ...\\n        ],\\n        [  # list of trials for session 2\\n            ...\\n        ],\\n        ...\\n    ],\\n\\n    'input': [  # list of sessions\\n        [  # list of trials for session 1\\n            input_data,  # stimulus/task variables, shape: (n_input, n_timepoints) or (n_input)\\n            ...\\n        ],\\n        ...\\n    ],\\n\\n    'output': [  # list of sessions\\n        [  # list of trials for session 1\\n            output_data,  # behavioral response, shape (n_output, n_timepoints) or (n_output)\\n            ...\\n        ],\\n        ...\\n    ],\\n\\n    'subjects': list of str, # Document each iteration: what was found, what was fixed, what the re-check showed\\n\\nWrite a report to CONVERSION_NOTES.md describing **every** check you did, to help convince the user that the conversion code works. **Done when**: You have documented your review findings and all issues are resolved in CONVERSION_NOTES.md under \\\"Step 10\\\".\\n\\n**\\u26a0\\ufe0f DO NOT PROCEED** to Step 11 until you have checked for consistency between **every** statistic in the reference texts, code, and data and documented in CONVERSION_NOTES.md under Step 10. \\n\\n---\\n\\n### Step 11: Full Decoder Training\\n\\n**Goal**: Validate the decoder on the **complete** dataset.\\n\\n**\\u26a0\\ufe0f IMPORTANT**: This step must complete fully. Do not skip or abbreviate. Do not proceed to Step 12 until this step is completely finished.\\n\\n**Actions**:\\n1. Run `python -u train_decoder.py converted_data.pkl --plot-samples 2>&1 | tee train_decoder_full_out.txt`. If the GPU does not have sufficient RAM for the network training, use the flag `--cpu` to specify to use the CPU to train. \\n2. **Wait for complete execution** \\u2014 this may take significant time for large datasets\\n3. **Check training**: Verify loss decreases over epochs\\n4. **Check accuracy**: High accuracy is an indicator that data conversion has been done correctly. Accuracy near chance is a sign that there might be issues in temporal alignment of signals, choice of data streams, or filtering or processing of data. Compare accuracy to expectations based on the papers. \\n\\n**Done when**: \\n1. Full decoder training completes (the script finishes running)\\n2. Accuracy results are documented in CONVERSION_NOTES.md under \\\"Step 11\\\" with a table of accuracies\\n\\n---\\n\\n### Step 12: Critical Review 2\\n\\n**Goal**: Find and fix any errors by performing the following checks. Fix issues and re-run affected steps. Iterate until no issues remain. Perform all of the following checks:\\n\\n**Check 1: Accuracy vs chance analysis**\\n- For each output, compare validation accuracy to chance (1/num_classes)\\n- Accuracy below chance indicates a bug, investigate and fix conversion issues to improve this. \\n- Accuracy below 1.5x chance may also indicate a bug, investigate whether changes in conversion logic could improve accuracy. \\n\\n**Check 2: Accuracy comparison to papers**\\n- Find every decoding accuracy reported in the reference papers\\n- Create a table comparing your accuracy to the papers's accuracy for each variable\\n- If your accuracy is lower than the reported accuracy, this may indicate a bug in your conversion that you must fix. \\n- Differences in algorithm/architecture should be relatively small because of the data dimensionality and size. Do not use this as an excuse to dismiss differences, investigate them completely before moving on. \\n\\n**Check 3: Train vs validation gap**\\n- If training accuracy is much higher (>1.5x) than validation accuracy for any output, investigate overfitting or data leakage. \\n\\n**If accuracy is low for an output, try these specific debugging steps:**\\n1. Verify the output values are correct by loading raw data and checking 3 specific trials\\n2. Check temporal alignment \\u2014 plot neural + output for a single trial to verify they're synchronized\\n3. Check whether the output has enough variation (not 99% one class)\\n4. Check that filtering steps for neural activity streams are followed. \\n5. Check that processing of the data matches the reference code and papers. \\n\\n**Iteration protocol**: If ANY check above reveals an issue:\\n1. Fix the issue in convert_data.py\\n2. Re-run the affected conversion and validation steps\\n3. Re-run ALL checks in this step (not just the one that failed)\\n4. Document each iteration: what was found, what was fixed, what the re-check showed\\n\\n**Report all checks and results** to `CONVERSION_NOTES.md` to convince the user that the conversion and decoding has been done correctly. \\n\\n**Done when**: You have documented your review findings and all issues are resolved in CONVERSION_NOTES.md under \\\"Step 12\\\".\\n\\n---\\n\\n### Step 13: Documentation and Cleanup\\n\\n**Goal**: Finalize documentation and organize files.\\n\\n**Actions**:\\n- Ensure CONVERSION_NOTES.md is complete with all:\\n  - Key decisions and rationale\\n  - Checks for correctness of the conversion\\n  - Validation results and tables\\n  - Any issues found and how they were resolved\\n- Create a user-facing `README.md` summarizing:\\n  - Dataset description\\n  - How to load and use the converted data\\n  - Output format specification\\n  - Key statistics\\n- Move analysis/investigation scripts to a `cache/` folder\\n- Create `cache/README_CACHE.md` documenting cached files\\n\\n**Done when**: README.md exists, CONVERSION_NOTES.md is complete, and directory is clean.\\n\\n---\\n\\n## CONVERSION_NOTES.md Template\\n\\n**Create this file IMMEDIATELY in Step 0. Trial number in block is derived from the `probabilityLeft` column in `_ibl_trials.table.pqt`.\n\nii.\n```python\ntrial_in_block = trial_number_in_block(trials_df['probabilityLeft'].to_numpy())\n```\n\niii. The AI uses changes in `probabilityLeft` to detect block boundaries, then counts trials within each block.\n\n## 4-b. What processing is involved in computing `input` *Trial number in block*?\n\ni. The AI iterates through the `probabilityLeft` array. When the value changes from the previous trial, it indicates a new block, and the counter resets to 1. Otherwise, the counter increments. The result is a 1-indexed count of the trial's position within its block.\n\nii.\n```python\ndef trial_number_in_block(prob_left):\n    out = np.zeros(len(prob_left), dtype=np.float32)\n    if len(prob_left) == 0:\n        return out\n    cur = prob_left[0]\n    c = 0\n    for i, p in enumerate(prob_left):\n        if i == 0 or p != cur:\n            cur = p\n            c = 1\n        else:\n            c += 1\n        out[i] = c\n    return out\n```\n\niii. The AI noted this is a reasonable derivation from the block structure in the task. The value is broadcast across all time bins for each trial as a per-trial constant.\n\n## 5-a. What variables in the raw data is `output` *Choice* derived from?\n\ni. Choice is derived from the `choice` column in `_ibl_trials.table.pqt`.\n\nii.\n```python\nchoice = map_choice(trials_df['choice'].to_numpy())\n```\n\niii. The AI documented that the IBL convention uses 1 for left and -1 for right in the raw data.\n\n## 5-b. What processing is involved in computing `output` *Choice*?\n\ni. The IBL choice encoding (1=left, -1=right) is remapped to the target encoding (left=0, right=1). Trials with other values (e.g., 0 for no-go) are marked as invalid (-1) and filtered out via the `valid` mask.\n\nii.\n```python\ndef map_choice(vals):\n    vals = np.asarray(vals)\n    out = np.full(vals.shape, -1, dtype=np.int64)\n    out[vals == 1] = 0   # left\n    out[vals == -1] = 1  # right\n    return out\n```\n\niii. The AI documented the IBL sign convention and the mapping to the decoder's binary encoding.\n\n## 6-a. What variables in the raw data is `output` *Prior probability of left* derived from?\n\ni. Prior probability of left is derived from the `probabilityLeft` column in `_ibl_trials.table.pqt`.\n\nii.\n```python\nprior = map_prior(trials_df['probabilityLeft'].to_numpy())\n```\n\niii. Straightforward extraction from the trials table.\n\n## 6-b. What processing is involved in computing `output` *Prior probability of left*?\n\ni. The continuous probability values are mapped to categorical indices: 0.2 -> 0, 0.5 -> 1, 0.8 -> 2. `np.isclose` is used for comparison to handle floating-point imprecision. Trials with other values are marked as invalid (-1) and filtered out.\n\nii.\n```python\ndef map_prior(vals):\n    vals = np.asarray(vals)\n    out = np.full(vals.shape, -1, dtype=np.int64)\n    out[np.isclose(vals, 0.2)] = 0\n    out[np.isclose(vals, 0.5)] = 1\n    out[np.isclose(vals, 0.8)] = 2\n    return out\n```\n\niii. The AI noted this matches the decoder task specification exactly.\n\n## 7-a. What variables in the raw data is `output` *Wheel speed* derived from?\n\ni. Wheel speed is derived from `_ibl_wheel.timestamps.npy` (timestamps) and `_ibl_wheel.position.npy` (position).\n\nii.\n```python\ndef load_wheel(session_alf: Path):\n    tp = session_alf / '_ibl_wheel.timestamps.npy'\n    pp = session_alf / '_ibl_wheel.position.npy'\n    return np.load(tp), np.load(pp)\n```\n\niii. The AI documented that the reference code also uses wheel position and timestamps.\n\n## 7-b. What processing is involved in computing `output` *Wheel speed*?\n\ni. Wheel velocity is computed from position using `np.gradient`. The velocity is then interpolated to the trial's time bin centers using `np.interp`. Duplicate timestamps are removed before gradient computation to avoid division-by-zero errors.\n\nii.\n```python\ndef interp_wheel_speed(timestamps, position, stim_on):\n    # ... dedup timestamps, handle NaNs ...\n    vel = np.gradient(position, timestamps)\n    trials = []\n    for t0 in stim_on:\n        x = t0 + TIME_CENTERS\n        y = np.interp(x, timestamps, vel, left=np.nan, right=np.nan)\n        trials.append(y.astype(np.float32))\n    return trials\n```\n\niii. The AI noted that the reference code uses wheel speed/velocity and bins it to 20 ms windows. The AI uses `np.gradient` for differentiation and `np.interp` for temporal alignment.\n\n## 7-c. How is `output` *Wheel speed* thresholded into categories?\n\ni. Wheel speed is discretized into 3 bins (low=0, mid=1, high=2) using tertile thresholds computed across all valid values within a session. The thresholds are the 1/3 and 2/3 quantiles of the pooled finite wheel speed values for that session.\n\nii.\n```python\ndef discretize_tertiles(list_of_arrays):\n    allv = np.concatenate([x[np.isfinite(x)] for x in list_of_arrays if np.isfinite(x).any()])\n    q1, q2 = np.quantile(allv, [1/3, 2/3]) if len(allv) else (0.0, 1.0)\n    out = []\n    for x in list_of_arrays:\n        y = np.zeros_like(x, dtype=np.int64)\n        y[x > q1] = 1\n        y[x > q2] = 2\n        y[~np.isfinite(x)] = 0\n        out.append(y)\n    return out, (float(q1), float(q2))\n```\n\niii. Trials with other values are marked as invalid (-1) and filtered out.\n\nii.\n```python\ndef map_prior(vals):\n    vals = np.asarray(vals)\n    out = np.full(vals.shape, -1, dtype=np.int64)\n    out[np.isclose(vals, 0.2)] = 0\n    out[np.isclose(vals, 0.5)] = 1\n    out[np.isclose(vals, 0.8)] = 2\n    return out\n```\n\niii.
